On the left, a term of $E_{p,k}(X;z)$ contains this monomial exactly when $\{i_{p-n+1},\ldots,i_p\}=\{k-n+1,\ldots,k\}$, and then the remaining factors $\prod_{j\leq p-n}(X_{i_j}-z_{i_j-j+1})$ with $i_1<\cdots<i_{p-n}\leq k-n$ are precisely the terms of $E_{p-n,k-n}(X_1,\ldots,X_{k-n};z)$. Hence, for every set $A$ of $k-n$ indices,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ereduction}
\sum_{j=0}^{p}c_{p,k,j}(z)\,e_{j-n}(y_A)=E_{p-n,\,k-n}(y_A;z),
\end{equation}
both sides being $0$ when $n>p$.

Now put $z_1=-t$ in Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}. By Lemma \ref{lemma:qelemkk} the left side becomes $\sum_{j=0}^k t^{k-j}\,\qsch_u(x;y)\,e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$, while on the right $\wte(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)=\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,w)}(y_a+t)=\sum_i t^{\,k-n_k(u,w)-i}e_i(y_{\fx_k(u,w)})$, since $|\fx_k(u,w)|=k-n_k(u,w)$. Comparing the coefficients of $t^{k-j}$ gives, for $0\leq j\leq k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonfactorialpieri}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)=\sum_{u\tom{k}w}q_k(u,w)\,e_{j-n_k(u,w)}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,w)}\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{equation}
Multiplying \eqref{eq:nonfactorialpieri} by $c_{p,k,j}(z)$, summing over $j$ and using \eqref{eq:qEexpansion} and then \eqref{eq:Ereduction} with $A=\fx_k(u,w)$ and $n=n_k(u,w)$ yields the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Iterated quantum Pieri formula] \label{corollary:iteratedpieri}
For all $u\in S_\infty$, $k\geq 1$ and $0\leq p_1,p_2\leq k$, and for two independent alphabets $z,z'$,
\begin{multline*}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{p_1,k}(x;z)\,\qelem_{p_2,k}(x;z')\\
=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w}
q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,
E_{p_1-n_k(u,v),\,k-n_k(u,v)}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,v)};z\big)\,
E_{p_2-n_k(v,w),\,k-n_k(v,w)}\big(y_{\fx_k(v,w)};z'\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y),
\end{multline*}
the sum being over the pairs with $n_k(u,v)\leq p_1$ and $n_k(v,w)\leq p_2$. In particular, for $p_1=p_2=k$,
\[
\qsch_u(x;y)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z')
=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w}
q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)
\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)
\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z'_1)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{theorem:pierigeneral} to multiply $\qsch_u(x;y)$ by
$\qelem_{p_1,k}(x;z)$, and then, for each resulting term indexed by $v$, apply it again with the $z$-alphabet replaced by $z'$. The coefficient
factors multiply, and the two successive relations are exactly
$u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The doubled formula}

Let $\theta$ be the quantization map of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov, introduced in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}: it is linear over the coefficient variables, it sends each standard elementary monomial $\prod_j e_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$ to $\prod_j e^{(q)}_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$, and $\theta(\sch_w(x;z))=\qsch_w(x;z)$. In particular $\theta(E_{p,k}(x;z))=\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$, and more generally $\theta$ is multiplicative on a product of factorial elementary polynomials $E_{p_j,k_j}(x;z^{(j)})$, $j=1,2,\ldots$, whose numbers of variables $k_j$ are \emph{pairwise distinct}, since such a product expands into standard elementary monomials. It is \emph{not} multiplicative when two factors have the same number of variables: in general$$\theta\big(E_{p_1,k}(x;z)E_{p_2,k}(x;z')\big)\neq\qelem_{p_1,k}(x;z)\,\qelem_{p_2,k}(x;z').$$ The following definition isolates the terms of Corollary \ref{corollary:iteratedpieri} which survive in the quantization of the product.

\begin{definition}[Compatible pairs of chains] \label{def:compatible}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$. We say that the pair of chains is \emph{compatible} if for every nontrivial cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ and every nontrivial cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ with $\max c=\max d$, one has $c\cap d=\{\max d\}$. In words: a cycle of the second step which shares its top with a cycle of the first step may meet that cycle only in the top. (A cycle of $v^{-1}w$ whose top is not the top of any cycle of $u^{-1}v$ is unrestricted.)
\end{definition}

Throughout this subsection we write $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)$ and $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ for the quantum factorial elementary polynomial $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\theta\big(\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)\big)$ and for the same polynomial with $z_1$ replaced by $z_2$; thus $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ is $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z')$ for the shifted alphabet $z'=(z_2,z_3,\ldots)$. For a pair of chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ put
$$\omega_k(u,v,w)=q_k(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2),$$
so that by Corollary \ref{corollary:iteratedpieri} (with $p_1=p_2=k$ and $z'=(z_2,z_3,\ldots)$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allk}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w}\omega_k(u,v,w)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{equation}
Recall that $d_2[k]$ denotes the dominant permutation with $\code(d_2[k])=(2^k)$, whose diagram is a $k\times2$ rectangle, so that
$$\sch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)(x_i-z_2)=E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2),\qquad \qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\theta\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2)\big);$$
we set $d_2[0]=\mathrm{id}$.

\begin{theorem}[Doubled quantum Pieri formula; proved for $k\leq2$, and for $k\geq3$ modulo Conjecture \ref{conjecture:incompatible}] \label{theorem:doubledpieri}
For $u\in S_\infty$ and $k\geq1$,
\begin{multline*}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\\ \text{compatible}}}\omega_k(u,v,w)\,\qsch_w(x;y)\\
=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\\ \text{compatible}}}q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{multline*}
\end{theorem}

Thus the doubled formula is obtained from the iterated formula \eqref{eq:allk} by \emph{omitting} the incompatible pairs, exactly as the quantization $\qsch_{d_2[k]}$ of the product $E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ omits terms from the product $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ of the quantizations. This is the rule implemented by the routine \texttt{double\_elem\_sym\_q} of \cite{schubmult}, and it is the ``paired step'' of the quantum algorithm of Section \ref{section:algorithms}. We have verified the theorem by computer for all $u\in S_4$ and all $k\leq3$; in these cases the unrestricted sum \eqref{eq:allk} fails to equal the left-hand side in roughly half of the instances, so the compatibility condition is essential.

\begin{remark}[Lower degrees] \label{remark:lowerdegrees}
We state the theorem only for the top degrees $p_1=p_2=k$, i.e.\ for the product with $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)$, which is the case that matters for the algorithm. Treating $z_1,z_2$ as indeterminates and extracting coefficients exactly as in the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} (the Leibniz rule for the products $\prod_{a\in A}(y_a-z_1)$) one obtains from it the corresponding formulas for $\qsch_u\,\theta\big(E_{p_1,k}(x;z)E_{p_2,k}(x;z')\big)$, with coefficients $E_{p_1-n_k(u,v),k-n_k(u,v)}(y_{\fx_k(u,v)};z)\,E_{p_2-n_k(v,w),k-n_k(v,w)}(y_{\fx_k(v,w)};z')$ summed over the compatible pairs; this is the general form implemented in \texttt{double\_elem\_sym\_q}, and we do not pursue it further.
\end{remark}

We now prove an \emph{alternating} version of the doubled formula unconditionally (Theorem \ref{theorem:alternatingdoubled}), and reduce the positive version, Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}, to a single combinatorial identity about pairs of chains, Conjecture \ref{conjecture:incompatible} below, which we have verified by computer but for which we do not have a proof; the reduction (Proposition \ref{prop:doubledreduction}) is rigorous, the identity is proved for $k=1$ (Lemma \ref{lemma:J1}) and $k=2$ (Proposition \ref{prop:J2}), and Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} is therefore established for $k\leq2$ and for every $k$ for which Conjecture \ref{conjecture:incompatible} holds. We also give a termwise reformulation (Conjecture \ref{conjecture:termwise}) based on the quantum Giambelli formula, and we prove that the pairs of chains organize into blocks along which the termwise identity telescopes (Lemma \ref{lemma:blocks}), so that it is equivalent to an identity between the ends of the blocks and the mixed chains that move the position $k+1$ (Proposition \ref{prop:junction}).

\subsubsection*{The recurrence}

The quantization of two equal columns is governed by the following recurrence, in which only powers of $q$ appear.

\begin{lemma}[Quantization of two equal columns] \label{lemma:thetaPP}
For all $k\geq1$,
$$\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)=\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)+q_k\,\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}(x;z),$$
and consequently
$$\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-j}q_{j+1}q_{j+2}\cdots q_k\;\qelem_{j,j}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{j,j}(x;z_2).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $P_k(z_1)=\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)=E_{k,k}(x;z)$ and $Q_k(z_1)=\theta(P_k(z_1))=\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)$, and similarly with $z_2$, with $P_0=Q_0=1$ and $P_{-1}=Q_{-1}=0$; thus $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)$ equals $\theta(P_k(z_1)P_k(z_2))$. Since $P_{k+1}(z_2)=(x_{k+1}-z_2)P_k(z_2)$, we have
$$P_k(z_1)P_{k+1}(z_2)-P_{k+1}(z_1)P_k(z_2)=(z_1-z_2)\,P_k(z_1)P_k(z_2).$$
The products $P_k(z_1)P_{k+1}(z_2)$ and $P_{k+1}(z_1)P_k(z_2)$ are $\mathbb Z[z_1,z_2]$-linear combinations of the standard elementary monomials $e_a(x_{\leq k})e_b(x_{\leq k+1})$, so $\theta$ acts on them multiplicatively, and
$$(z_1-z_2)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=W_k,\qquad W_k:=Q_k(z_1)Q_{k+1}(z_2)-Q_{k+1}(z_1)Q_k(z_2).$$
The three-term recursion $Q_{k+1}(z_2)=(x_{k+1}-z_2)Q_k(z_2)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_2)$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:elemrecursion} gives
\begin{multline*}
W_k=Q_k(z_1)\big[(x_{k+1}-z_2)Q_k(z_2)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_2)\big]-\big[(x_{k+1}-z_1)Q_k(z_1)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_1)\big]Q_k(z_2)\\
=(z_1-z_2)Q_k(z_1)Q_k(z_2)-q_kW_{k-1}.
\end{multline*}
Dividing by $z_1-z_2$ (all terms are divisible, $W_k$ being antisymmetric in $z_1,z_2$) gives $\qsch_{d_2[k]}=Q_kQ_k-q_k\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}$, which is the first identity, and iterating it gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A Pieri-theoretic proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP}] \label{remark:pieriproofrecursion}
The recurrence can also be read off from the Pieri formula itself. Apply Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri} to $u=c[k,k]=[2,3,\ldots,k+1,1]$, whose quantum double Schubert polynomial is $\qelem_{k,k}(x;y)$, with a second alphabet $z$:
$$\qelem_{k,k}(x;y)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\sum_{c[k,k]\tom{k}w}q_k(c[k,k],w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(c[k,k],w)}(y_a-z_1)\,\qsch_w(x;y).$$
One checks that every nonempty set $\fx_k(c[k,k],w)$ contains the value $2=u(1)$, and that the only $w$ with $\fx_k(c[k,k],w)=\emptyset$ are $w=d_2[k]$, with $q_k(c[k,k],w)=1$, and $w=d_2[k-1]$, with $q_k(c[k,k],w)=q_k$ (we have verified both statements for $k\leq5$). Substituting $z_1=y_2$ therefore kills every coefficient of positive degree and leaves $\qelem_{k,k}(x;y_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;y_2)=\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;y)+q_k\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}(x;y)$, which is the recurrence with $z$ renamed $y$. For $k=3$ the expansion reads, with permutations written by their codes,
\begin{align*}
\qelem_{3,3}(x;y)\qelem_{3,3}(x;z)&=(y_2-z_1)(y_3-z_1)(y_4-z_1)\,\qsch_{(1,1,1)}+(y_2-z_1)(y_3-z_1)\,\qsch_{(1,1,2)}\\
&\quad+(y_2-z_1)\,\qsch_{(1,2,2)}+\qsch_{(2,2,2)}+q_3\big[(y_2-z_1)(y_3-z_1)\,\qsch_{(1,1)}+(y_2-z_1)\,\qsch_{(1,2)}+\qsch_{(2,2)}\big].
\end{align*}
\end{remark}

Multiplying the second identity of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} by $\qsch_u(x;y)$ and expanding each product by \eqref{eq:allk} gives an unconditional, if alternating, doubled Pieri formula.

\begin{theorem}[Alternating doubled Pieri formula] \label{theorem:alternatingdoubled}
For $u\in S_\infty$ and $k\geq1$,
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-j}q_{j+1}\cdots q_k\sum_{u\tom{j}v\tom{j}w}\omega_j(u,v,w)\,\qsch_w(x;y).\eqno\qed$$
\end{theorem}

Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} asserts that this alternating sum collapses onto its $j=k$ term restricted to the compatible pairs. The first identity of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} makes this precise as a recurrence in $k$. For $u,w\in S_\infty$ let $\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)$, $\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)$ and $\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)$ denote the sum of $\omega_k(u,v,w)$ over all pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, over the compatible pairs, and over the incompatible pairs, respectively, so that $\mathrm{All}_k=\mathrm{Comp}_k+\mathrm{Inc}_k$ and, by \eqref{eq:allk}, $\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)=[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)\big)$; here $\mathrm{Comp}_0(u,w)=\mathrm{All}_0(u,w)=\delta_{u,w}$.

\begin{proposition}[Reduction] \label{prop:doubledreduction}
Suppose Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds with $k$ replaced by $k-1$ (for $k=1$ this is the trivial statement $\qsch_u\qsch_{d_2[0]}=\qsch_u$). Then Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Jk}
\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)=q_k\,\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w)\qquad\text{for all }u,w\in S_\infty.\tag{$\mathrm J_k$}
\end{equation}
Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for all $k\leq K$ if and only if \eqref{eq:Jk} holds for all $k\leq K$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP}, \eqref{eq:allk} and the hypothesis,
$$[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}\big)=\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)-q_k[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}\big)=\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)-q_k\,\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w),$$
while $\mathrm{Comp}_k=\mathrm{All}_k-\mathrm{Inc}_k$. The two sides of Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} therefore differ by $\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)-q_k\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w)$. The last assertion follows by induction on $k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The Monk induction] \label{remark:monkinduction}
One might hope to prove Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} by an induction in the position $k$ parallel to the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}, avoiding \eqref{eq:Jk}. The relevant recurrence is obtained from Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} and Lemma \ref{lemma:elemrecursion}:
\begin{multline*}
\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=(x_k-z_2)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k-1,k-1}(x;z_2)\\
+q_{k-1}\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k-2,k-2}(x;z_2)-q_k\,\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}(x;z).
\end{multline*}
Multiplying by $\qsch_u(x;y)$ and expanding $\qsch_u\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)=\sum_vc_k(u,v)\qsch_v$, the first two terms become
$$\sum_vc_k(u,v)\big[(x_k-z_2)\,\qsch_v\,\qelem_{k-1,k-1}(x;z_2)+q_{k-1}\,\qsch_v\,\qelem_{k-2,k-2}(x;z_2)\big],$$
and the inductive step of the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri} (equation \eqref{eq:pieriexpand} with $u$ replaced by $v$) shows that the coefficient of $\qsch_w$ in the bracket is $c_k(v,w)$. Hence the Monk cancellation reproduces $\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)$ and the induction reduces to \eqref{eq:Jk} again: the Monk summands are indexed by the second chain alone, whereas compatibility is a property of the pair of chains. The machinery of Section \ref{section:proofpieri} is nevertheless the natural language for a proof of \eqref{eq:Jk}, as the case $k=2$ below illustrates: the letter $t_{k,k+1}$, which is present in every incompatible pair and is the quantum Monk neighbour carrying the weight $q_k$, is the pivot of the detours.
\end{remark}

The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} says that the terms omitted from the iterated Pieri formula at level $k$ are exactly accounted for, with an extra factor $q_k$, by the doubled formula one level down. Its left-hand side is a sum over pairs of chains whose cycles overlap, and the following lemma shows that such an overlap forces a quantum step.

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:incompatquantum}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ and suppose that a cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ and a cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ have the same top $B$ and share an element $a\leq k$. Then at least one of $c$ (as a $u$-tight cycle) and $d$ (as a $v$-tight cycle) has a descent, i.e.\ contains a quantum step. Consequently every term of $\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)$ is divisible by $q_k$, and \eqref{eq:Jk} holds after setting $q=0$; in particular the doubled formula specializes at $q=0$ to the classical iterated Pieri formula, as it must.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $c=(a_p,\ldots,a_1,B)$ with values $\beta_0=u(B)$, $\beta_j=u(a_j)$, and $d=(b_r,\ldots,b_1,B)$ with values $\gamma_0=v(B)$, $\gamma_j=v(b_j)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv) we may process $c$ first among the cycles of $u^{-1}v$, so $v(B)=\beta_p$ and $v(a_i)=\beta_{i-1}$; and $d$ first among the cycles of $v^{-1}w$. Suppose neither cycle has a descent, so that by Corollary \ref{corollary:cyclic} $\beta_0>\beta_1>\cdots>\beta_p$ and $\gamma_0>\gamma_1>\cdots>\gamma_r$. If $a=a_i=b_j$ with $i,j\geq1$, then $\gamma_j=v(a_i)=\beta_{i-1}>\beta_p=\gamma_0>\gamma_j$, a contradiction. For the consequence, note that a quantum step $t_{ab}$ of a level-$k$ chain has $a\leq k<b$, so $q_{ab}=q_a\cdots q_{b-1}$ is divisible by $q_k$; and at $q=0$ both sides of \eqref{eq:Jk} vanish.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Overlapping cycles have top $k+1$] \label{lemma:overlap}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, and suppose that a cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ and a cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ have the same top $B$ and share an element $a\leq k$. Then $B=k+1$, and moreover $k\in c\cup d$, i.e.\ one of the two cycles contains the transposition $t_{k,k+1}$. Consequently a pair of chains in which either step contains no transposition $t_{\cdot,k+1}$ is compatible, and in an incompatible pair both steps contain a cycle with top $k+1$, these two cycles overlap, no other pair of cycles does, and the letter $t_{k,k+1}$ occurs in one of the two chains.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We show more generally that there is no position $r\notin c\cup d$ with $a'<r<B$ for every $a'\in(c\cup d)\setminus\{B\}$; applied to $r=k+1$ this gives $B=k+1$ (as $k+1\notin c\cup d$ when $B\geq k+2$), and applied to $r=k$ it gives $k\in c\cup d$. Suppose such an $r$ exists. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv) and Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} the fixed sets and weights of the two chains do not depend on the order in which their cycles are processed, and every order gives a chain of covers; we process $c$ last in the first step and $d$ first in the second. Write $u'$ for the permutation obtained from $u$ by applying the other cycles of $u^{-1}v$, so that $v=u'c$ and $c$ is $u'$-tight, and write $c=(a_p,\ldots,a_1,B)$, $\beta_0=u'(B)=u(B)$, $\beta_l=u'(a_l)=u(a_l)$, and $d=(b_r,\ldots,b_1,B)$, $\gamma_0=v(B)$, $\gamma_m=v(b_m)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(i), $v(B)=\beta_p$ and $v(a_l)=\beta_{l-1}$ for $1\leq l\leq p$; as $a=a_i=b_j$ with $i,j\geq1$, we get
$$\gamma_0=\beta_p,\qquad \gamma_j=\beta_{i-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad \beta_{i-1}\neq\beta_p.$$
Let $\eta$ be the value at the position $r$ when the letters of $c$ are applied. Since $r\notin c\cup d$ and nothing happens between the last letter of $c$ and the first letter of $d$, $\eta$ is also the value at position $r$ when the letters of $d$ are applied. Moreover $a_l<r<B$ for every $l$ and $b_m<r<B$ for every $m$, so the conditions \eqref{eq:Cj} and \eqref{eq:Qj} apply to the position $r$ at every step of $c$ and of $d$; for $c$ they read: $\eta\notin(\beta_l,\beta_{l-1})$ if $\beta_l<\beta_{l-1}$, and $\eta\in(\beta_{l-1},\beta_l)$ if $\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}$, for $1\leq l\leq p$. By Corollary \ref{corollary:cyclic} the cyclic sequence $\Gamma=(\beta_0,\ldots,\beta_p)$ is unimodal, and the displayed conditions say precisely that $\eta$ lies in the gap $(\beta_p,\beta_0)$ of $\Gamma$: if $\Gamma$ has no descent then $\eta\notin(\beta_p,\beta_0)$, i.e.\ $\eta<\beta_p=\min\Gamma$ or $\eta>\beta_0=\max\Gamma$; and if the ascent of $\Gamma$ is $(\beta_{q-1},\beta_q)$ then $\eta\in(\min\Gamma,\max\Gamma)$ and $\eta$ avoids all the descent gaps $(\beta_l,\beta_{l-1})$ with $l\neq q$, which together with $(\beta_0,\beta_p)$ cover $(\min\Gamma,\max\Gamma)\setminus\Gamma$, so $\beta_0<\eta<\beta_p$. In other words, in the cyclically decreasing arrangement of $\Gamma\cup\{\eta\}$ the value $\eta$ sits between $\beta_p$ and $\beta_0$. The same argument for $d$ shows that in the cyclically decreasing arrangement of $\Gamma'\cup\{\eta\}$, $\Gamma'=(\gamma_0,\ldots,\gamma_r)$, the value $\eta$ sits between $\gamma_r$ and $\gamma_0=\beta_p$.

If $\eta>\beta_p$, the first statement forces $\beta_p=\min\Gamma$ and $\eta>\max\Gamma$, while the second forces $\Gamma'\cap(\beta_p,\eta)=\emptyset$; but $\beta_{i-1}\in\Gamma\setminus\{\beta_p\}$ lies in $(\beta_p,\eta)$ and belongs to $\Gamma'$, a contradiction. If $\eta<\beta_p$, the second statement forces $\eta=\min(\Gamma'\cup\{\eta\})$ and $\beta_p=\max\Gamma'$, while the first forces $\Gamma\cap(\eta,\beta_p)=\emptyset$; but $\beta_{i-1}\in\Gamma'\setminus\{\beta_p\}$ lies in $(\eta,\beta_p)$ and belongs to $\Gamma$, again a contradiction. This proves the claim, hence $B=k+1$ and $k\in c\cup d$. The consequences follow because a step contains at most one cycle with a given top.
\end{proof}

The second ingredient of the detours is the following general form of the two detours of $t_{12}$ met in the case $k=2$ (Lemma \ref{lemma:detour} below). For a permutation $\rho$ and $a<k$ put $x=\rho(a)$, $y=\rho(k)$, $z=\rho(k+1)$, and say that $\rho$ is \emph{even} or \emph{odd at $(a,k)$} according to the parity of the pattern of $(x,y,z)$.

\begin{lemma}[Detours of $t_{ak}$] \label{lemma:generaldetour}
Let $\rho\in S_\infty$ and $a<k$, and consider the two words
$$E=t_{k,k+1}\,t_{a,k+1}\,t_{k,k+1},\qquad O=t_{a,k+1}\,t_{k,k+1}\,t_{a,k+1},$$
both equal to $t_{ak}$. Then $E$ is a path of covers from $\rho$ if and only if $t_{ak}$ is a cover of $\rho$ and $\rho$ is even at $(a,k)$, and $O$ is a path of covers from $\rho$ if and only if $t_{ak}$ is a cover of $\rho$ and $\rho$ is odd at $(a,k)$. In either case the weight of the path is $q_k\,\kappa(\rho,t_{ak})$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $J=\{\rho(r):a<r<k\}$; the positions $a,k,k+1$ carry $(x,y,z)$ and the letters of $E$ and $O$ only permute these three values, leaving $J$ in place. By Definition \ref{def:cover}, $t_{ak}$ is a cover of $\rho$ iff $(x,y)$ is $J$-admissible, and $t_{k,k+1}$ is a cover of every permutation. Along $E$ the values at $(a,k,k+1)$ are $(x,y,z)\to(x,z,y)\to(y,z,x)\to(y,x,z)$, so $E$ is a path of covers iff $(x,y)$ is $(J\cup\{z\})$-admissible, i.e.\ (Lemma \ref{lemma:admissible}(i)) iff $(x,y)$ is $J$-admissible and $\{z\}$-admissible; and $(x,y)$ is $\{z\}$-admissible exactly when $x<y$ and $z\notin(x,y)$ or $x>y$ and $z\in(y,x)$, i.e.\ when the pattern of $(x,y,z)$ is even. Along $O$ the values are $(x,y,z)\to(z,y,x)\to(z,x,y)\to(y,x,z)$, so $O$ is a path of covers iff $(x,z)$ is $(J\cup\{y\})$-admissible and $(z,y)$ is $(J\cup\{x\})$-admissible. If so, then $(z,y)$ is $J$-admissible and Lemma \ref{lemma:admissible}(iii) (with $\gamma=z$, $\beta=y$, $\alpha=x$, $I=J$) shows that $(x,y)$ is $J$-admissible, while $\{y\}$-admissibility of $(x,z)$ says that the pattern of $(x,z,y)$ is even, i.e.\ that of $(x,y,z)$ is odd. Conversely, if $(x,y)$ is $J$-admissible and the pattern is odd, the three odd orders $x<z<y$, $z<y<x$, $y<x<z$ are checked directly: in the first, $J\cap(x,y)=\emptyset$ gives $(J\cup\{y\})\cap(x,z)=\emptyset$ and $(J\cup\{x\})\cap(z,y)=\emptyset$; in the second, $J\subseteq(y,x)$ gives $J\cup\{y\}\subseteq(z,x)$ and $(J\cup\{x\})\cap(z,y)=\emptyset$; in the third, $J\subseteq(y,x)$ gives $(J\cup\{y\})\cap(x,z)=\emptyset$ and $J\cup\{x\}\subseteq(y,z)$. For the weights, the cover types along $E$ are determined by the signs of $y-z$, $x-y$, $z-x$ and along $O$ by those of $x-z$, $y-x$, $z-y$; in each of the three even, respectively odd, orders exactly one of the two letters $t_{k,k+1}$, or else the middle letter, is quantum when $t_{ak}$ is classical, and the quantum letter $t_{a,k+1}$ of weight $q_{a,k+1}=q_{ak}q_k$ occurs, with the two others classical, when $t_{ak}$ is quantum; in all cases the weight is $q_k\kappa(\rho,t_{ak})$.
\end{proof}

Lemma \ref{lemma:overlap} makes rigorous the first of the two observations recorded after Conjecture \ref{conjecture:incompatible} below, and the lifting part of the bijection predicted by \eqref{eq:Bk}; it also identifies the letter $t_{k,k+1}$, the quantum Monk neighbour of weight $q_k$, as the pivot of every incompatible pair (cf.\ Remark \ref{remark:monkinduction}).

\begin{lemma}[The case $k=1$] \label{lemma:J1}
The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} holds for $k=1$: $\mathrm{Inc}_1(u,w)=q_1\delta_{u,w}$. Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k=1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A level-$1$ chain is either trivial or a single transposition $t_{1B}$, $B\geq2$. A pair $u\tom1v\tom1w$ with a trivial step has no overlapping cycles and is compatible, so an incompatible pair has $v=ut_{1B}$ and $w=vt_{1B'}$ with the cycles $\{1,B\}$ and $\{1,B'\}$ sharing their top, i.e.\ $B'=B$ and $w=u$; conversely every such pair is incompatible, as the two cycles meet in $1$. We claim that $B=2$. For any $v$ and $B$, the length change $\ell(vt_{1B})-\ell(v)$ equals $\pm(1+2m)$, where $m$ is the number of positions $1<j<B$ whose value lies between $v(1)$ and $v(B)$, the sign being $+$ if $v(1)<v(B)$. If $u\lup ut_{1B}$ is a classical cover then $u(1)<u(B)$ and $m=0$; in $v=ut_{1B}$ the values at $1$ and $B$ are exchanged, so $\ell(vt_{1B})=\ell(v)-1$, and this is a quantum cover only if $-1=-2(B-1)+1$, i.e.\ $B=2$. If $u\lup ut_{1B}$ is a quantum cover then $u(1)>u(B)$ and $-(1+2m)=-2(B-1)+1$, so $m=B-2$, every intermediate position having a value between $u(B)$ and $u(1)$; then $\ell(vt_{1B})=\ell(v)+1+2(B-2)$, which is a classical cover only if $B=2$. Thus the incompatible pairs with source $u$ are exactly $u\tom1ut_{12}\tom1u$, with one classical and one quantum step; the weight is $q_{12}=q_1$, and both fixed sets are empty since position $1$ is moved in each step. Hence $\mathrm{Inc}_1(u,w)=q_1\delta_{u,w}=q_1\mathrm{Comp}_0(u,w)$, and Proposition \ref{prop:doubledreduction} gives the last assertion.
\end{proof}


\subsubsection*{The case $k=2$}

We now prove \eqref{eq:Jk} for $k=2$. The proof is a model for what a proof of the general case has to accomplish: the incompatible pairs at level $2$ turn out to be exactly the compatible pairs at level $1$ in which one step---a transposition $t_{12}$ or the empty step---has been replaced by a ``detour'' through the position $3$, carrying the extra factor $q_2$.

Throughout, for a permutation $\rho$ and $a<b$ we say that $t_{ab}$ is a \emph{classical cover at $\rho$} if $\ell(\rho t_{ab})=\ell(\rho)+1$ and a \emph{quantum cover at $\rho$} if $\ell(\rho t_{ab})=\ell(\rho)-2(b-a)+1$, and we put $\kappa_{ab}(\rho)=1$, respectively $\kappa_{ab}(\rho)=q_{ab}$; if neither holds, $t_{ab}$ is \emph{not a cover} at $\rho$. Since $\ell(\rho t_{ab})-\ell(\rho)=\pm(1+2m)$, where $m$ is the number of positions $a<j<b$ with $\rho(j)$ between $\rho(a)$ and $\rho(b)$ and the sign is that of $\rho(b)-\rho(a)$, we have the following criterion, which we use constantly.

\begin{lemma}[Cover criterion] \label{lemma:covercriterion}
$t_{ab}$ is a classical cover at $\rho$ if and only if $\rho(a)<\rho(b)$ and no $\rho(j)$, $a<j<b$, lies in the open interval $(\rho(a),\rho(b))$; it is a quantum cover at $\rho$ if and only if $\rho(a)>\rho(b)$ and every $\rho(j)$, $a<j<b$, lies in $(\rho(b),\rho(a))$. In particular the cover type of $t_{ab}$ at $\rho$ depends only on $\rho(a)$, $\rho(b)$ and the \emph{set} $\{\rho(j):a<j<b\}$. Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] If $t_{ab}$ is a cover at $\rho$ and at $\rho'$, where $\rho'(a)=\rho(b)$, $\rho'(b)=\rho(a)$ and $\{\rho'(j):a<j<b\}=\{\rho(j):a<j<b\}$, then $b=a+1$; and for $b=a+1$ the transposition $t_{a,a+1}$ is a cover at every permutation, classical or quantum according as $\rho(a)<\rho(a+1)$ or $\rho(a)>\rho(a+1)$.
\item[(ii)] For every $\rho$ the word $t_{23}t_{23}$ is a path of covers from $\rho$ back to $\rho$ with exactly one quantum step, of total weight $q_2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The criterion restates the length formula; (i) is the argument of Lemma \ref{lemma:J1} (if the first cover is classical the second requires $1=2(b-a)-1$, and if it is quantum the second requires $1+2(b-a-1)=1$); (ii) is the case $a=2$, $b=3$ of (i).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Level $1$] \label{lemma:comp1}
For $u,w\in S_\infty$,
$$\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,w)=\begin{cases}(y_{u(1)}-z_1)(y_{u(1)}-z_2)&\text{if }w=u,\\[2pt]
\kappa_{1b}(u)\big[(y_{u(1)}-z_1)+(y_{u(b)}-z_2)\big]&\text{if }w=ut_{1b},\ b\geq2,\ t_{1b}\text{ a cover at }u,\\[2pt]
\kappa_{1b}(u)\,\kappa_{1b'}(ut_{1b})&\text{if }u^{-1}w\text{ is the $3$-cycle }1\mapsto b'\mapsto b\mapsto1,\ b\neq b',\\
&\quad t_{1b}\text{ a cover at }u\text{ and }t_{1b'}\text{ a cover at }ut_{1b},\\[2pt]
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A level-$1$ chain is trivial or a single cover $t_{1b}$, and by Lemma \ref{lemma:J1} the only incompatible level-$1$ pairs are $u\tom1ut_{12}\tom1u$. The pair (trivial, trivial) gives the first line; the pairs (trivial, $t_{1b}$) and ($t_{1b}$, trivial) give the two terms of the second line, with fixed values $u(1)$, respectively $w(1)=u(b)$; and ($t_{1b}$, $t_{1b'}$) with $b\neq b'$ gives the third line, where $u^{-1}w=t_{1b}t_{1b'}$ is the $3$-cycle $1\mapsto b'\mapsto b\mapsto1$, which determines $b$ and $b'$. The three cases are disjoint, and no other $w$ occurs.
\end{proof}

The incompatible pairs at level $2$ are described by the next three lemmas. We write a level-$2$ chain as a word in the transpositions $t_{1b},t_{2b}$ ($b\geq3$) read from left to right, so that a pair of chains is a word with a cut, and we call the letters $t_{13},t_{23}$ the \emph{core letters} and the letters $t_{ab}$ with $b\geq4$ the \emph{spectators}. Recall that within a step the $b$'s are weakly decreasing and the $a$'s are distinct, so a step contains at most two letters, and a step containing a spectator contains at most one core letter, which comes after the spectator and uses the other position.

\begin{lemma}[The overlapping cycles have top $3$] \label{lemma:topthree}
Let $u\tom2v\tom2w$ be incompatible, and let $c$ (a cycle of $u^{-1}v$) and $d$ (a cycle of $v^{-1}w$) have the same top $B$ and share an element $a\in\{1,2\}$. Then $B=3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the case $k=2$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:overlap}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Shapes] \label{lemma:shapes}
Let $u\tom2v\tom2w$ be incompatible and let $S_i\subseteq\{1,2\}$ be the set of $a$ such that $t_{a3}$ occurs in step $i$. Then $S_1\cap S_2\neq\emptyset$, and the word formed by the core letters, with its cut, is one of
\begin{align*}
&\mathrm{K}_0=t_{23}\,|\,t_{23},\qquad \mathrm{K}_1=t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{23},\qquad \mathrm{K}_1'=t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13},\\
&\mathrm{K}_2=t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{13},\qquad \mathrm{K}_2'=t_{13}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13},\qquad \mathrm{K}_3=t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{23},\qquad \mathrm{K}_3'=t_{23}\,|\,t_{13}t_{23},\\
&\mathrm{K}_4=t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{13}t_{23},\qquad \mathrm{K}_4'=t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13}.
\end{align*}
A step with two core letters contains nothing else; a step with one core letter $t_{a3}$ may in addition begin with one spectator $t_{a'b}$, $a'\neq a$, $b\geq4$. Every spectator position $b$ lies in the support of $u^{-1}w$, and if both steps contain spectators (which happens only for $\mathrm K_0$) their positions are distinct. The products of the words are: $e$ for $\mathrm K_0$; $t_{13}$ for $\mathrm K_1,\mathrm K_1'$; $t_{12}$ for $\mathrm K_2,\mathrm K_2',\mathrm K_3,\mathrm K_3'$; $t_{13}t_{12}$ for $\mathrm K_4$ and $t_{12}t_{13}$ for $\mathrm K_4'$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:topthree} the overlapping cycles are $S_1\cup\{3\}$ and $S_2\cup\{3\}$, and they share an element of $\{1,2\}$, so $S_1\cap S_2\neq\emptyset$. The constraints on the letters of a step were recalled above. It remains to exclude some core words. If $S_1=S_2=\{1\}$, the two letters $t_{13}$ are separated at most by a spectator $t_{2b}$; writing $(x,y,z)$ for the values at $(1,2,3)$ when the first $t_{13}$ is applied and $y'$ for the value at position $2$ when the second is applied, Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion} gives, if the first is classical, $x<z$, $y\notin(x,z)$ and $y'\in(x,z)$, while $t_{2b}$ at $(z,y,x)$ is classical only if $y<y'$ and $x\notin(y,y')$ and quantum only if $y>y'$ and $x\in(y',y)$, both impossible; the case of a quantum first letter is symmetric. The same computation excludes $t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{13}$ and $t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{2b}t_{13}$, and Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}(i) excludes $t_{13}\,|\,t_{13}t_{23}$, $t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13}$ and $t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{13}t_{23}$, in which two letters $t_{13}$ are separated by nothing or by $t_{23}t_{23}$, which preserves the value at position $2$. What remains is the list. Two spectators at the same position $b\geq4$ can only occur in $\mathrm K_0$, as $t_{1b}t_{23}\,|\,t_{1b}t_{23}$, and are excluded by Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}(i) since $t_{23}$ preserves the set of values at positions $2,\ldots,b-1$; hence every spectator position lies in the support of $u^{-1}w$. The products are immediate from $t_{13}t_{23}t_{13}=t_{23}t_{13}t_{23}=t_{12}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Detours of $t_{12}$] \label{lemma:detour}
Let $\rho$ be a permutation and let $D_1=t_{13}t_{23}t_{13}$, $D_2=t_{23}t_{13}t_{23}$, both equal to $t_{12}$. Exactly one of $D_1$, $D_2$ is a path of covers from $\rho$ to $\rho t_{12}$, namely $D_1$ if the pattern of $(\rho(1),\rho(2),\rho(3))$ is an odd permutation ($132$, $213$ or $321$) and $D_2$ if it is even ($123$, $231$ or $312$); its weight is $q_2\,\kappa_{12}(\rho)$. The intermediate permutations are $\rho t_{13}$, $\rho t_{13}t_{23}$ for $D_1$ and $\rho t_{23}$, $\rho t_{23}t_{13}$ for $D_2$; in particular the values at the position not moved by the one-letter part of each cut are, for $D_1$, $\rho(2)$ at position $2$ after the first letter and $\rho(1)$ at position $2$ after the second, and for $D_2$, $\rho(1)$ at position $1$ after the first letter and $\rho(2)$ at position $1$ after the second.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is the case $a=1$, $k=2$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:generaldetour}, with $D_1=O$ and $D_2=E$; note that $t_{12}$ is a cover of every permutation. For the reader's convenience we also record the direct verification. Write $(x,y,z)$ for the values at $(1,2,3)$. For $D_1$ the first letter is a cover iff $x<z<y$ (classical) or $z<y<x$ (quantum) or $y<x<z$ (classical), and in these cases the remaining two letters are covers of types $(q,c)$, $(c,c)$, $(c,q)$ respectively, by Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}; the total weight is $q_2$, $q_1q_2$, $q_1q_2$, which is $q_2\kappa_{12}(\rho)$ since $\kappa_{12}(\rho)=1$ iff $x<y$. For $D_2$ the first letter is always a cover, and the second is a cover iff $z<x<y$ (classical), $x<y<z$ (classical) or $y<z<x$ (quantum), with third letters of types $c$, $q$, $c$ and total weights $q_2$, $q_2$, $q_1q_2$. The six order types are thus split three and three, and the intermediate permutations are read off from the words.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The case $k=2$] \label{prop:J2}
The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} holds for $k=2$. Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k\leq2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix $u$ and write $x=u(1)$, $y=u(2)$, $z=u(3)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:comp1} we must show that $\mathrm{Inc}_2(u,w)$ equals $q_2$ times the expression of that lemma. By Lemma \ref{lemma:shapes} the incompatible pairs with source $u$ are the words listed there, with at most one spectator per step, and $\pi=u^{-1}w$ is accordingly $e$, $t_{1b}$ ($b\geq2$), or a $3$-cycle $t_{1b}t_{1b'}$; in particular $\mathrm{Inc}_2(u,w)=0$ whenever $\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,w)=0$, and we go through the remaining cases. We say that a level-$2$ word and a level-$1$ word \emph{match} if they are simultaneously paths of covers, with level-$2$ weight equal to $q_2$ times the level-$1$ weight.

\emph{$\pi=e$.} The only word is $\mathrm K_0$ without spectators, $u\tom2ut_{23}\tom2u$, which is always a path of covers of weight $q_2$ (Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}(ii)) and fixes the value $x$ at position $1$ in both steps. Its contribution $q_2(y_x-z_1)(y_x-z_2)$ is $q_2\,\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,u)$.

\emph{$\pi=t_{12}$.} The words are $\mathrm K_2,\mathrm K_2'$ (the two cuts of $D_1$) and $\mathrm K_3,\mathrm K_3'$ (the two cuts of $D_2$), without spectators. By Lemma \ref{lemma:detour} exactly one detour is a path of covers, of weight $q_2\kappa_{12}(u)$, and its two cuts are both admissible (each step has distinct $a$'s and equal $b$'s) and incompatible. The fixed values are $\{y\}$, $\emptyset$ and $\emptyset$, $\{x\}$ for the two cuts of $D_1$, and $\{x\}$, $\emptyset$ and $\emptyset$, $\{y\}$ for those of $D_2$, so in either case $\mathrm{Inc}_2(u,ut_{12})=q_2\kappa_{12}(u)\big[(y_x-z_1)+(y_y-z_2)\big]=q_2\,\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,ut_{12})$.

\emph{$\pi=t_{13}$.} The words are $\mathrm K_1=t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}$ and $\mathrm K_1'=t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13}$ without spectators. In $\mathrm K_1$ the letter $t_{13}$ is applied at $u$ and is followed by $t_{23}t_{23}$, always a path of weight $q_2$; the fixed values are $\emptyset$ and $\{z\}$ (the value at position $1$ after $t_{13}t_{23}$). In $\mathrm K_1'$ the letters $t_{23}t_{23}$ return to $u$ and $t_{13}$ is again applied at $u$; the fixed values are $\{x\}$ and $\emptyset$. Both match $t_{13}$ at $u$, and the sum is $q_2\kappa_{13}(u)[(y_x-z_1)+(y_z-z_2)]=q_2\,\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,ut_{13})$.

\emph{$\pi=t_{1b}$, $b\geq4$.} The words are $\mathrm K_0$ with one spectator, $t_{1b}t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}$ and $t_{23}\,|\,t_{1b}t_{23}$. In the first, $t_{1b}$ is applied at $u$; in the second, at $ut_{23}$, where the values $u(1)$, $u(b)$ and the set of values at positions $2,\ldots,b-1$ are the same as at $u$, so the cover type is the same by Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}. The fixed values are $\emptyset,\{u(b)\}$ and $\{x\},\emptyset$, and the sum is $q_2\kappa_{1b}(u)[(y_x-z_1)+(y_{u(b)}-z_2)]=q_2\,\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,ut_{1b})$.

\emph{$\pi=t_{1b}t_{1b'}$ with $b,b'\geq4$.} The only word is $t_{1b}t_{23}\,|\,t_{1b'}t_{23}$, with both fixed sets empty; $t_{1b}$ is applied at $u$ and $t_{1b'}$ at $ut_{1b}t_{23}$, where the values at positions $1$ and $b'$ and the set of values at the positions between them agree with those at $ut_{1b}$. Hence the word matches the level-$1$ pair $t_{1b}\,|\,t_{1b'}$, and $\mathrm{Inc}_2(u,w)=q_2\kappa_{1b}(u)\kappa_{1b'}(ut_{1b})=q_2\,\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,w)$.

\emph{$\pi=t_{13}t_{1b}$ or $t_{1b}t_{13}$ with $b\geq4$.} The words are $\mathrm K_1$ with the spectator $t_{1b}$ in the second step, $t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{1b}t_{23}$, and $\mathrm K_1'$ with the spectator in the first step, $t_{1b}t_{23}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13}$; the fixed sets are empty. In the first, $t_{1b}$ is applied at $ut_{13}t_{23}$, which agrees with $ut_{13}$ at position $1$ and on the set of values at positions $2,\ldots,b-1$; in the second, $t_{13}$ is applied at $ut_{1b}t_{23}t_{23}=ut_{1b}$. So they match $t_{13}\,|\,t_{1b}$ and $t_{1b}\,|\,t_{13}$ respectively.

\emph{$\pi=t_{13}t_{12}$ or $t_{12}t_{13}$.} The words are $\mathrm K_4=t_{13}\cdot D_2$ with the cut after the first letter of $D_2$, and $\mathrm K_4'=D_2\cdot t_{13}$ with the cut after the second letter of $D_2$; the fixed sets are empty, and the level-$1$ pairs are $t_{13}\,|\,t_{12}$ and $t_{12}\,|\,t_{13}$. In $\mathrm K_4$ the detour $D_2$ is applied at $ut_{13}$, and in $\mathrm K_4'$ at $u$, and $t_{13}$ is applied at $u$, respectively at $ut_{12}$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:detour}, $D_2$ is a path of covers at a permutation $\rho$ exactly when the pattern of $(\rho(1),\rho(2),\rho(3))$ is even, and then has weight $q_2\kappa_{12}(\rho)$; one checks directly from Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion} that if $t_{13}$ is a cover at $u$ then the pattern of $ut_{13}$ at positions $1,2,3$ is even, and that if the pattern of $u$ at positions $1,2,3$ is even then $t_{13}$ is a cover at $ut_{12}$ (in both cases the three values $x,y,z$ satisfy $x<z<y$ or $y<x<z$ or $z<y<x$ up to the relevant exchange). Hence $\mathrm K_4$ matches $t_{13}\,|\,t_{12}$ and $\mathrm K_4'$ matches $t_{12}\,|\,t_{13}$, and no other words contribute to these $\pi$. (When the pattern of $u$ is odd, both sides vanish for $\pi=t_{12}t_{13}$: $D_1$ is the detour at $u$, and the words $D_1\cdot t_{13}$ are excluded by Lemma \ref{lemma:shapes}, while $t_{13}$ is not a cover at $ut_{12}$.)

\emph{$\pi=t_{12}t_{1b}$ or $t_{1b}t_{12}$ with $b\geq4$.} These are the only cases in which the matching changes the spectator: the words are, for $\pi=t_{1b}t_{12}$, $\mathrm K_2$ with spectator, $t_{13}t_{23}\,|\,t_{2b}t_{13}$, and $\mathrm K_3'$ with spectator, $t_{1b}t_{23}\,|\,t_{13}t_{23}$; and for $\pi=t_{12}t_{1b}$, $\mathrm K_2'$ with spectator, $t_{2b}t_{13}\,|\,t_{23}t_{13}$, and $\mathrm K_3$ with spectator, $t_{23}t_{13}\,|\,t_{1b}t_{23}$ (here $t_{13}t_{23}t_{2b}t_{13}=t_{12}t_{2b}=t_{1b}t_{12}$ and $t_{2b}t_{12}=t_{12}t_{1b}$). All fixed sets are empty, so we must show that the sum of the weights of the level-$2$ words equals $q_2$ times the weight of the level-$1$ pair $t_{1b}\,|\,t_{12}$, respectively $t_{12}\,|\,t_{1b}$. The $\mathrm K_3,\mathrm K_3'$ words match directly: in $\mathrm K_3$, $D_2$ is applied at $u$ and $t_{1b}$ at $ut_{23}t_{13}$, which agrees with $ut_{12}$ at position $1$ and on the set of values at positions $2,\ldots,b-1$; in $\mathrm K_3'$, $t_{1b}$ is applied at $u$ and $D_2$ at $ut_{1b}t_{23}t_{23}=ut_{1b}$. They are present exactly when the pattern of $u$, respectively of $ut_{1b}$, at positions $1,2,3$ is even. The $\mathrm K_2,\mathrm K_2'$ words involve the spectator $t_{2b}$, whose cover condition at a permutation $\rho$ compares $\rho(2)$ with $\rho(b)$ and involves the values at positions $3,\ldots,b-1$, whereas the level-$1$ letter $t_{1b}$ compares $\rho'(1)$ with $\rho'(b)$ and involves positions $2,\ldots,b-1$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion} and Lemma \ref{lemma:detour}, the validity and the weights of all the words in this case, as well as of the level-$1$ pairs, depend only on the relative order of the four values $x,y,z,\beta=u(b)$ and on which of the five intervals they cut out contain some value $u(j)$, $3<j<b$; this leaves $24\cdot2^5$ configurations, and for each of them one verifies the identity by Lemma \ref{lemma:covercriterion}. For instance, if $x<z<y$ (so that $D_1$ is the detour at $u$, of weight $q_2$) and $\beta<x$ with every $u(j)$, $3<j<b$, in $(\beta,y)$, the level-$1$ pair $t_{12}\,|\,t_{1b}$ has weight $1\cdot q_{1b}$ (a classical $t_{12}$ followed by a quantum $t_{1b}$ at $(y,x,z,\ldots,\beta)$), while on the level-$2$ side $\mathrm K_3$ is absent, and in $\mathrm K_2'$ the spectator $t_{2b}$ is a quantum cover at $u$ of weight $q_{2b}$ and the detour at $ut_{2b}$, whose pattern $(x,\beta,z)$ is odd, is $D_1$ with weight $q_2\kappa_{12}(ut_{2b})=q_2q_1$; the total $q_1q_2q_{2b}=q_2q_{1b}$ agrees. We have carried out this finite verification by computer, in the stronger form that for every one of the $24\cdot 2^5$ configurations (realized by a permutation of at most $9$ letters, with one value in each occupied interval), and also for the $6$ configurations without spectator, the full identity $\mathrm{Inc}_2(u,w)=q_2\mathrm{Comp}_1(u,w)$ holds for all $w$, where $\mathrm{Inc}_2$ is computed from all level-$2$ chains without using Lemma \ref{lemma:shapes}; this also re-checks all the preceding cases.

This exhausts the possible $\pi$ by Lemma \ref{lemma:shapes}, and proves \eqref{eq:Jk} for $k=2$. The last assertion follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:J1} and Proposition \ref{prop:doubledreduction}.
\end{proof}

The proof exhibits the mechanism behind \eqref{eq:Jk}: every incompatible pair at level $2$ is a compatible pair at level $1$ in which either the empty step has been replaced by $t_{23}t_{23}$ or the letter $t_{12}$ by one of its two detours $t_{13}t_{23}t_{13}$, $t_{23}t_{13}t_{23}$ through the position $3$, with the cut between the two Pieri steps placed inside the detour; the detour always contains exactly one more quantum step than the letter it replaces, which accounts for the factor $q_2$, and the values fixed at position $2$ during the detour are the values fixed at position $1$ before and after the original letter, in the reverse order for $D_1$ (which is why \eqref{eq:Jk} is not termwise). For general $k$ the analogous statement would replace a cycle $C$ with top $k$ of a level-$(k-1)$ chain by the conjugate $t_{k,k+1}\,C'\,t_{k,k+1}$, where $C'$ is $C$ with top $k+1$, or by the conjugate of $C'$ by one of the letters $t_{a,k+1}$; we have not carried this out.

\begin{conjecture} \label{conjecture:incompatible}
The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} holds for all $k\geq3$ and all $u,w\in S_\infty$.
\end{conjecture}

We have verified \eqref{eq:Jk} by computer for all $u\in S_6$ and all $1\leq k\leq4$, with the chains confined to $S_6$ (59\,526 pairs $(u,w)$), and Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} directly for all $u\in S_4$ and $k\leq3$. By Proposition \ref{prop:doubledreduction}, a proof of Conjecture \ref{conjecture:incompatible} completes the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}. Two features of \eqref{eq:Jk} that any proof must account for are the following. First, by Lemma \ref{lemma:overlap}, in every incompatible pair the overlapping cycles have top $B=k+1$ and one of them contains $k$, so that both steps contain a transposition $t_{a,k+1}$ and one of them contains $t_{k,k+1}$; the incompatibility is a ``detour'' of the level-$(k-1)$ transposition $t_{ak}$ through the position $k+1$, in accordance with $t_{a,k+1}t_{k,k+1}t_{a,k+1}=t_{ak}$. Second, \eqref{eq:Jk} is not a termwise identity: already for $u=132$, $k=2$, $w=312$ the left-hand side is $q_2\big[(y_3-z_1)+(y_1-z_2)\big]$ and the right-hand side is $q_2\big[(y_1-z_1)+(y_3-z_2)\big]$. In all examples the pairs with given $(u,w)$ and $q$-weight organize into chains $v_1,\ldots,v_{m+1}$ whose fixed sets are nested, with contributions $\prod_{j<i}(y_{c_j}-z_1)\prod_{j>i}(y_{c_j}-z_2)$, which sum to the divided difference $\big(\prod_j(y_{c_j}-z_1)-\prod_j(y_{c_j}-z_2)\big)/(z_2-z_1)$ and so depend only on the set $\{c_j\}$; the identity \eqref{eq:Jk} then amounts to a matching of these sets between the two levels. We now explain the origin of this divided-difference structure and use it to recast the conjecture as a termwise identity.

\subsubsection*{A termwise reformulation via the Giambelli formula}

The proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} shows that
$$(z_1-z_2)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_2)-\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2),$$
which is the quantum Giambelli formula for the $k\times2$ rectangle written as a divided difference. Expanding both products on the right by Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri} at the two levels $k$ and $k+1$ gives the following reformulation. For $u,w\in S_\infty$ put
\begin{align*}
M_k(u,w)&=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w}q_k(u,v)\,q_{k+1}(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_{k+1}(v,w)}(y_b-z_2),\\
M'_k(u,w)&=\sum_{u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w}q_{k+1}(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_{k+1}(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2).
\end{align*}

\begin{proposition}[Giambelli form] \label{prop:giambelliform}
For every $k\geq1$ and all $u,w\in S_\infty$,
$$(z_1-z_2)\,[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)\big)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w).$$
Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ if and only if $(z_1-z_2)\,\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w)$ for all $u,w$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Multiply the displayed Giambelli identity by $\qsch_u(x;y)$ and expand $\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_2)$ by two applications of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}, first at level $k$ with the variable $z_1$ and then at level $k+1$ with the variable $z_2$; this gives $M_k$. The product $\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$, expanded first at level $k+1$ and then at level $k$, gives $M'_k$.
\end{proof}

The right-hand side of Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform} is a sum of products in which the levels of the two steps differ, and the factor $z_1-z_2$ on the left can be absorbed into the compatible pairs as follows. For a pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ let $c(v)=v(k+1)$. If the first step does not use the position $k+1$ then $c(v)=u(k+1)$, and otherwise $c(v)=u(a)$ for a position $a\leq k$ moved by the first step; in either case $c(v)\notin\fx_k(u,v)$, and $c(v)\notin\fx_k(v,w)\subseteq\{v(1),\ldots,v(k)\}$. Hence, writing $z_1-z_2=(y_{c(v)}-z_2)-(y_{c(v)}-z_1)$,
\begin{multline*}
(z_1-z_2)\,\omega_k(u,v,w)\\
=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\Big[\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}}(y_b-z_2)-\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\}}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2)\Big],
\end{multline*}
and each of the two products on the right has the same shape as a term of $M_k$ or $M'_k$. Writing $\mathcal T(u,w)$ for the multiset of triples $\big(q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w),\ \fx_k(u,v),\ \fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}\big)$ over the compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, $\mathcal S(u,w)$ for the multiset of $\big(q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w),\ \fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\},\ \fx_k(v,w)\big)$ over the same pairs, $\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ for the multiset of $\big(q_k(u,v)q_{k+1}(v,w),\fx_k(u,v),\fx_{k+1}(v,w)\big)$ over the chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ and $\mathcal M'_k(u,w)$ for the corresponding multiset over the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$, we are led to the following termwise statement, from which Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} follows immediately by Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform}.

\begin{conjecture}[Termwise form] \label{conjecture:termwise}
For all $k\geq1$ and $u,w\in S_\infty$ there is an equality of multisets
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bk}
\mathcal T(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M'_k(u,w)=\mathcal S(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M_k(u,w).\tag{$\mathrm B_k$}
\end{equation}
\end{conjecture}

We have verified \eqref{eq:Bk} for all $u\in S_5$ and $1\leq k\leq5$, with the chains confined to $S_8$ (about $80\,000$ compatible pairs), and \eqref{eq:Bk} implies Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}, hence \eqref{eq:Jk}, for the same $k$. Unlike \eqref{eq:Jk}, the identity \eqref{eq:Bk} is an equality of multisets, so it predicts a bijection. We now prove that the greater part of this bijection is forced: the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$ fall into explicit \emph{blocks}, inside which the terms of $\mathcal T$ and $\mathcal S$ cancel telescopically, and the unblocked ends of the blocks are matched with the chains of $\mathcal M_k$ and $\mathcal M'_k$ that do not move the position $k+1$. This reduces \eqref{eq:Bk} to an identity between the blocked ends of the blocks and the mixed chains that do move $k+1$ (Proposition \ref{prop:junction}), and in the simplest situation that identity is exactly the up/down pairing of Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} (Remark \ref{remark:elementaryjunction}).

\subsubsection*{Blocks, and the reduction of \eqref{eq:Bk} to its junctions}

For a pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ we write $[A\mid B]=\prod_{a\in A}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in B}(y_b-z_2)$ and
$$T(v)=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,[\fx_k(u,v)\mid\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}],\qquad S(v)=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,[\fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\}\mid\fx_k(v,w)],$$
so that $(z_1-z_2)\,\omega_k(u,v,w)=T(v)-S(v)$ by the display above, and $\mathcal T(u,w)$, $\mathcal S(u,w)$ are the multisets of the $T(v)$, $S(v)$ over the compatible pairs (we identify the monomial $q[A\mid B]$ with the triple $(q,A,B)$). Recall from Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv) and Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} that the cycles of a step may be processed in any order.

\begin{definition}[Block data, cut, block] \label{def:blockdata}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ be compatible, $\pi_1=u^{-1}v$, $\pi_2=v^{-1}w$. Let $c$ be the cycle of $\pi_1$ with top $k+1$ and $d$ the cycle of $\pi_2$ with top $k+1$ (either may be trivial). By compatibility $c\cap d\subseteq\{k+1\}$, so writing $c=(x_i,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$ and $d=(x_n,\ldots,x_{i+1},k+1)$ we have, by \eqref{eq:pivot},
$$X:=cd=t_{x_1,k+1}\cdots t_{x_n,k+1}=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1),$$
a cycle with top $k+1$ which we call the \emph{merged cycle} of the pair. Let $\rho_1=\pi_1c^{-1}$ and $\rho_2=d^{-1}\pi_2$ be the products of the remaining cycles of $\pi_1$ and of $\pi_2$; all of these have tops $\geq k+2$, and $u^{-1}w=\rho_1X\rho_2$. We call $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ the \emph{block data} and $i$ the \emph{cut} of the pair, and we put
$$i_{\min}=\max\big(\{j:x_j\in\supp\rho_2\}\cup\{0\}\big),\qquad i_{\max}=\min\big(\{j:x_j\in\supp\rho_1\}\cup\{n+1\}\big)-1.$$
Since the cycles of $\pi_1$ are disjoint, $x_1,\ldots,x_i\notin\supp\rho_1$, and likewise $x_{i+1},\ldots,x_n\notin\supp\rho_2$; hence $i_{\min}\leq i\leq i_{\max}$. The \emph{block} of the pair is the set of compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v'\tom{k}w$ with the same block data; the blocks partition the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Blocks] \label{lemma:blocks}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ be compatible with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$, $X=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$, and put $u'=u\rho_1$, $c_j=(x_j,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$, $d_j=(x_n,\ldots,x_{j+1},k+1)$ and $v_j=u'c_j$ for $0\leq j\leq n$, so that $v=v_i$ where $i$ is the cut. Then the block of the pair is $\{v_j: i_{\min}\leq j\leq i_{\max}\}$, and for every $j$ in this range:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $u\tom{k}v_j\tom{k}w$ is a compatible pair with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and cut $j$, and $q_k(u,v_j)\,q_k(v_j,w)=q_k(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)$;
\item[(ii)] $\fx_k(u,v_j)=F_1\setminus\{u(x_1),\ldots,u(x_j)\}$ and $\fx_k(v_j,w)=F_2\setminus\{w(x_{j+1}),\ldots,w(x_n)\}$, where $F_1=\{u(a):a\leq k,\ a\notin\supp\rho_1\}$ and $F_2=\{w(a):a\leq k,\ a\notin\supp\rho_2\}$;
\item[(iii)] $c(v_j)=u(x_j)$ if $j\geq1$ and $c(v_0)=u(k+1)$; moreover $w(x_j)=c(v_{j-1})$ for $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently $S(v_j)=T(v_{j-1})$ for $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, and the contribution of the block telescopes:
$$(z_1-z_2)\sum_{j=i_{\min}}^{i_{\max}}\omega_k(u,v_j,w)=T(v_{i_{\max}})-S(v_{i_{\min}}).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) For $j\leq i_{\max}$ the positions $x_1,\ldots,x_j$ avoid $\supp\rho_1$, so $\rho_1c_j$ is a product of disjoint cycles, each with exactly one element greater than $k$; similarly $d_j\rho_2$ for $j\geq i_{\min}$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} and Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv), $u\tom{k}v_j\tom{k}w$ holds if and only if $\rho_1$ is $u$-tight, $c_j$ is $u'$-tight, $d_j$ is $v_j$-tight, and $\rho_2$ is $v_jd_j$-tight, where $v_jd_j=u'X$ does not depend on $j$. The first and the last condition do not involve $j$, and they hold because they hold for $j=i$ (as $\pi_1=\rho_1c$ is $u$-tight and $\pi_2=d\rho_2$ is $v$-tight). For the two middle conditions write $\beta_0=u'(k+1)$ and $\beta_l=u'(x_l)$ for $1\leq l\leq n$. The pivot factorization of $X$ is the concatenation of those of $c_j$ and $d_j$, so by Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(i) applied to $X$ and $u'$, the $l$-th letter $t_{x_l,k+1}$ exchanges the value $\beta_l$ at $x_l$ with the value $\beta_{l-1}$ at $k+1$, for every $l$ and independently of $j$; in particular $v_j(k+1)=\beta_j$ and $v_j(x_l)=\beta_l$ for $l>j$. Hence
$$\desc_{u'}(c_j)=\{x_l:l\leq j,\ \beta_l>\beta_{l-1}\},\qquad \desc_{v_j}(d_j)=\{x_l:l>j,\ \beta_l>\beta_{l-1}\},$$
and by Definition \ref{def:tight}
$$\Lw_{u'}(c_j)+\Lw_{v_j}(d_j)=n-2\sum_{l:\,\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}}(k+1-x_l)=\Lw_{u'}(X),\qquad \qw_{u'}(c_j)\,\qw_{v_j}(d_j)=\prod_{l:\,\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}}q_{x_l,k+1}=\qw_{u'}(X),$$
both independent of $j$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(ii),
$$\ell(u'X)-\ell(u')=\big(\ell(v_j)-\ell(u')\big)+\big(\ell(u'X)-\ell(v_j)\big)\geq\Lw_{u'}(c_j)+\Lw_{v_j}(d_j)=\Lw_{u'}(X),$$
with equality if and only if $c_j$ is $u'$-tight and $d_j$ is $v_j$-tight. Equality holds for $j=i$, hence for every $j$. By Corollary \ref{corollary:cycleweight} and Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv), $q_k(u,v_j)q_k(v_j,w)=\qw_u(\rho_1)\,\qw_{u'}(c_j)\,\qw_{v_j}(d_j)\,\qw_{u'X}(\rho_2)=\qw_u(\rho_1)\qw_{u'}(X)\qw_{u'X}(\rho_2)$, independent of $j$. The pair is compatible, since its cycles with tops $\geq k+2$ are those of $\rho_1$ and $\rho_2$, as for the given pair, and $c_j\cap d_j=\{k+1\}$; its block data is $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and its cut is $j$ by construction. Conversely every pair in the block has cut in $[i_{\min},i_{\max}]$ and is therefore one of the $v_j$, since the block data and the cut determine $\pi_1=\rho_1c_j$.

(ii) The positions $a\leq k$ moved by $\pi_1^{(j)}=\rho_1c_j$ are those of $\supp\rho_1$ together with $x_1,\ldots,x_j$, which gives the first formula (note $u(x_l)\in F_1$ for $l\leq i_{\max}$). A position $a\leq k$ is fixed by $d_j\rho_2$ if and only if $a\notin\supp\rho_2\cup\{x_{j+1},\ldots,x_n\}$, and then $v_j(a)=w(a)$; this gives the second formula (note $w(x_l)\in F_2$ for $l>i_{\min}$).

(iii) We saw that $v_j(k+1)=\beta_j$, which is $u'(x_j)=u(x_j)$ for $1\leq j\leq i_{\max}$ (as $x_j\notin\supp\rho_1$) and $u'(k+1)=u(k+1)$ for $j=0$ (as $k+1\notin\supp\rho_1$). Now let $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$ and consider the pair $v_{j-1}$. The position $x_j$ is not moved by its first step $\rho_1c_{j-1}$. Its second step is $d_{j-1}\rho_2$, whose first letter $t_{x_j,k+1}$ moves the value $v_{j-1}(k+1)=c(v_{j-1})$ to the position $x_j$, and $x_j$ is moved by no later letter: not by the remaining letters of $d_{j-1}$, and not by $\rho_2$ because $j>i_{\min}$. Hence $w(x_j)=c(v_{j-1})$.

For the consequence write $A_j=\fx_k(u,v_j)$ and $B_j=\fx_k(v_j,w)$. For $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, (ii) and (iii) give $A_j\cup\{c(v_j)\}=A_j\cup\{u(x_j)\}=A_{j-1}$ and $B_j=B_{j-1}\cup\{w(x_j)\}=B_{j-1}\cup\{c(v_{j-1})\}$, while the $q$-weights agree by (i); thus $S(v_j)=T(v_{j-1})$. Summing $(z_1-z_2)\omega_k(u,v_j,w)=T(v_j)-S(v_j)$ over the block, all terms cancel except $T(v_{i_{\max}})$ and $S(v_{i_{\min}})$.
\end{proof}

We call $v_{i_{\max}}$ the \emph{head} and $v_{i_{\min}}$ the \emph{tail} of the block; the head is \emph{blocked} if $i_{\max}<n$ (some $x_j$ lies in $\supp\rho_1$) and the tail is \emph{blocked} if $i_{\min}>0$ (some $x_j$ lies in $\supp\rho_2$). Thus a block is the set of all cuts of the merged word $t_{x_1,k+1}\cdots t_{x_n,k+1}$ that are not obstructed by a spectator cycle of $\rho_1$ or $\rho_2$ sharing a position with $X$, and the transit value $c(v_j)$ runs through the values of $u$ at $x_n,x_{n-1},\ldots,x_1,k+1$ as the cut moves from right to left. The block lemma is the general form of the ``two cuts of a detour'' met in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:J2}.

\begin{proposition}[Reduction of \eqref{eq:Bk} to junctions] \label{prop:junction}
Let $u,w\in S_\infty$. Let $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}(u,w)\subseteq\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ be the sub-multiset of the chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ whose second step moves the position $k+1$, let $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w)\subseteq\mathcal M'_k(u,w)$ consist of the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$ whose first step moves $k+1$, and let $\mathcal T^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)$, respectively $\mathcal S^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)$, be the multiset of $T(v)$ over the blocked heads $v$, respectively of $S(v)$ over the blocked tails $v$, of the blocks of compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$. Then \eqref{eq:Bk} holds for $(u,w)$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:junction}
\mathcal T^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w)=\mathcal S^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M^{\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w),\tag{$\mathrm J^{\mathrm B}_k$}
\end{equation}
and in that case $(z_1-z_2)\,\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w)$. In particular, Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ as soon as \eqref{eq:junction} holds for all $u,w\in S_\infty$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:blocks} the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$ are partitioned into blocks, and inside each block the elements $S(v_j)$, $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, of $\mathcal S(u,w)$ coincide with the elements $T(v_{j-1})$ of $\mathcal T(u,w)$. Cancelling them, \eqref{eq:Bk} is equivalent to
$$\{T(v):v\text{ a head}\}\uplus\mathcal M'_k(u,w)=\{S(v):v\text{ a tail}\}\uplus\mathcal M_k(u,w).$$
Now let $v=v_n$ be an unblocked head, so $d=1$ and $v(k+1)=w(k+1)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:leveldichotomy}(i), applied with $k+1$ in place of $k$, $v\tom{k+1}w$ with $q_{k+1}(v,w)=q_k(v,w)$ and $\fx_{k+1}(v,w)=\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{v(k+1)\}$; thus $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ is a chain of $\mathcal M_k(u,w)\setminus\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}(u,w)$, and its triple is $T(v)$. Conversely, let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ be a chain not moving $k+1$. By the same lemma $v\tom{k}w$ with the same weight, the second step has no cycle with top $k+1$, so the pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ is compatible with cut $i=n$; hence it is the head of its block, this head is unblocked ($x_1,\ldots,x_n$ avoid $\supp\rho_1$ as the cycles of $\pi_1$ are disjoint), and its triple is $T(v)$. Since distinct blocks have distinct heads, $v\mapsto v$ is a bijection between the unblocked heads and the chains of $\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ not moving $k+1$, preserving the triples. Symmetrically, the unblocked tails $v_0$ (for which $c=1$ and $u(k+1)=v_0(k+1)$) correspond bijectively to the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$ not moving $k+1$, with $S(v_0)=\big(q,\fx_{k+1}(u,v_0),\fx_k(v_0,w)\big)$. Cancelling these two families gives the equivalence with \eqref{eq:junction}. The remaining assertions follow from Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform}.
\end{proof}

The identity \eqref{eq:junction} concerns only the \emph{junctions} of the blocks: a blocked head $v_{i_{\max}}$, whose next cut is obstructed by a cycle $e$ of $\rho_1$ with top $B\geq k+2$ through $x_{i_{\max}+1}$, and a blocked tail $v_{i_{\min}}$, obstructed by a cycle of $\rho_2$ through $x_{i_{\min}}$, together with the mixed chains in which the position $k+1$ is moved by a cycle with top $B\geq k+2$. In all our computations the correspondence predicted by \eqref{eq:junction} is realised by \emph{transporting} the obstructing cycle $e$ across $X$: a blocked tail with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and $e\subseteq\rho_2$ through $x_j$, $j=i_{\min}$, is matched with the blocked head of the block with data $(\rho_1\cdot XeX^{-1},X,\rho_2e^{-1})$, in which $e$ has been conjugated into the first step and is attached to $x_{j-1}$ (so that the cut $j-1$ is skipped; if $j=1$ the transported cycle $XeX^{-1}$ contains $k+1$ and the partner is instead a chain of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k$), except that in some junctions pivots of $X$ migrate into the transported cycle or out of it, exactly as in the words $F_m$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:updown}; and a chain of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$, in which $e\ni k+1$, is matched in the same way with the head obtained by transporting $e$ to the first step, attached to $x_n$. We have verified \eqref{eq:junction}, and the block decomposition of Lemma \ref{lemma:blocks} pair by pair, for all $u\in S_5$ with chains in $S_6$ for $k=2$, for all $u\in S_6$ with chains in $S_7$ and all $u\in S_5$ with chains in $S_8$ for $k=3$ ($140\,080$ and $16\,224$ compatible pairs), and for all $u\in S_5$ with chains in $S_8$ for $k=4$ ($27\,467$ pairs). The simplest junction is already in this paper:

\begin{remark}[The elementary junction is Lemma \ref{lemma:updown}] \label{remark:elementaryjunction}
Let $u\in S_\infty$, let $x_1,\ldots,x_n\leq k<k+1<B$ be distinct, let $Y=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1,B)$ and $w=uY$, and apply Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} with $\psi=u$ and with $k+1,B,x_1,\ldots,x_n$ in the roles of $k,B,z_1,\ldots,z_s$. Its three families of words are, in the present language: $F_{\mathrm{up}}=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1)\cdot t_{k+1,B}$, the chain $u\tom{k}ut_{x_1,k+1}\cdots t_{x_n,k+1}\tom{k+1}w$ of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}(u,w)$ (the whole cycle $(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$ in the first step, the position $k+1$ moved by $t_{k+1,B}$ in the second); $F_{\mathrm{std}}$, the pivot factorization of $Y$, i.e.\ the chain $u\tom{k+1}w\tom{k}w$ of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w)$ with trivial second step; and $F_m=(x_{m-1},\ldots,x_1,k+1)\,(x_n,\ldots,x_m,B)\,t_{x_m,k+1}$, $1\leq m\leq n$, the compatible pair with block data $\big((x_n,\ldots,x_m,B),\ (x_m,\ldots,x_1,k+1),\ 1\big)$ and cut $m-1$: here the spectator $(x_n,\ldots,x_m,B)$ is attached to the merged cycle at $x_m$ and has absorbed the pivots $x_{m+1},\ldots,x_n$, so the pair is the head of its block and is blocked ($i_{\max}=m-1$). All three kinds of terms carry the same triple $\big(q,\ u[1,k]\setminus\{u(x_1),\ldots,u(x_n)\},\ w[1,k]\big)$, where $u[1,k]=\{u(a):a\leq k\}$ (for $F_m$ the transit value is $c(v)=v(k+1)=w(x_m)$, the one value of $w[1,k]$ not fixed by the second step), $q$ denoting the common weight. Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} says: (a) if $F_{\mathrm{std}}$ is a cover path then so is $F_{\mathrm{up}}$, with the same weight, and no $F_m$ is, so that this term of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k$ cancels against this term of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$ in \eqref{eq:junction}; (b) if $F_{\mathrm{up}}$ is a cover path but $F_{\mathrm{std}}$ is not, then exactly one $F_m$ is, with the same weight, so that this term of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$ is matched with exactly one blocked head; and (c) a blocked head of the shape $F_m$ never occurs without its partner $F_{\mathrm{up}}$. Thus Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} is the junction correspondence of \eqref{eq:junction} restricted to these three families (the cluster of $(u,w)$ contains further terms, in which the first step does not use all of $x_1,\ldots,x_n$); the matched block then runs from its head $F_m$ down to its unblocked tail $v_0=u\,(x_n,\ldots,x_m,B)$, which is matched with the chain $u\tom{k+1}v_0\tom{k}w$ of $\mathcal M'_k$ as in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:junction}. The general case of \eqref{eq:junction} requires the analogous analysis for an obstructing cycle with several elements below $k$, attached to an arbitrary pivot of $X$ or to $k+1$, and for several obstructing cycles, which may lie in both steps; we have not carried this out.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Specializations of $z_1$ and $z_2$] \label{remark:specialization}
Both sides of \eqref{eq:Jk} have degree at most $k-1$ in $z_1$ and in $z_2$ (a pair with $\lvert\fx_k(u,v)\rvert=k$ has $v=u$ and is compatible, and similarly for the second step), so by interpolation it suffices to prove \eqref{eq:Jk} after the specializations $z_1=y_{u(i)}$, $z_2=y_{u(j)}$, $1\leq i,j\leq k$, which retain exactly the pairs whose first step moves position $i$ and whose second step moves the value $u(j)$. For $u=c[k,k]$ and $i=1$ this specialization kills every term of positive degree (Remark \ref{remark:pieriproofrecursion}); for general $u$, however, no value is common to all the nonempty fixed sets, and the specialized identities are not termwise.

The situation is different on the diagonal $z_2=z_1$, that is, when both factors $\qelem_{k,k}$ are taken in the \emph{same} variable $z_1$. There the weight $\omega_k(u,v,w)$ depends only on the multiset $\fx_k(u,v)\uplus\fx_k(v,w)$, and we have verified, for all $u\in S_5$ and $k\leq5$ with chains in $S_7$, that \eqref{eq:Jk} becomes an identity of multisets:
\begin{multline}\label{eq:Dk}
\big\{\big(q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w),\ \fx_k(u,v)\uplus\fx_k(v,w)\big)\big\}_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\\\text{incompatible}}}\\
=\big\{\big(q_k\,q_{k-1}(u,v)q_{k-1}(v,w),\ \fx_{k-1}(u,v)\uplus\fx_{k-1}(v,w)\big)\big\}_{\substack{u\tom{k-1}v\tom{k-1}w\\\text{compatible}}},\tag{$\mathrm D_k$}
\end{multline}
whereas for every specialization with $z_1\neq z_2$ that we have tried (generic, or $z_1=y_c$, $z_2=y_{c'}$ with $c\neq c'$) the identity is not termwise; the example $u=132$, $k=2$, $w=312$ above already shows this, the two sides $q_2[(y_3-z_1)+(y_1-z_2)]$ and $q_2[(y_1-z_1)+(y_3-z_2)]$ agreeing termwise exactly when $z_1=z_2$. In particular the numbers of incompatible pairs at level $k$ and of compatible pairs at level $k-1$ with the same $(u,w)$ and the same $q$-weight coincide.

In fact \eqref{eq:Dk} holds in the strongest possible termwise sense. Call the \emph{key} of a pair $u\tom{m}v\tom{m}w$ the triple $\big(w,\ q_m(u,v)q_m(v,w),\ \fx_m(u,v)\uplus\fx_m(v,w)\big)$. We have verified, for all $u\in S_6$ with chains in $S_7$ for $k=3$ ($41\,254$ incompatible pairs) and in $S_8$ for $k=4$ ($154\,073$ pairs), that for fixed $u$ the keys of the incompatible level-$k$ pairs are pairwise distinct, that the keys of the compatible level-$(k-1)$ pairs are pairwise distinct, and that multiplying the weight by $q_k$ maps the second set of keys bijectively onto the first. Thus for each compatible pair $u\tom{k-1}v'\tom{k-1}w$ there is a \emph{unique} incompatible pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ with the same fixed-value multiset and $q_k$ times the weight, and this correspondence $v'\mapsto v$ is a bijection; \eqref{eq:Dk} would follow from a proof that the correspondence is well defined and surjective. Moreover, in the ``pure'' situation in which the level-$(k-1)$ pair has no cycles with top $k+1$ and no cycle with a larger top interacts with the detour, the bijection is given by two explicit detours generalizing Lemma \ref{lemma:detour}: if the top-$k$ cycles of the two steps have pivot sequences $C_1$ and $C_2$ (meeting only in $k$) and $P=C_1C_2=(p_1,\ldots,p_m)$ is their concatenation, then the image has top-$(k+1)$ cycles with pivot sequences either $c=(k)C_1$, $d=C_2(k)$ (the ``even'' form, the conjugate of $P$ by $t_{k,k+1}$ with the cut inside), or $c=P(k)$, $d=(p_1)$ when $C_1=\emptyset$ and $c=C_1$, $d=C_2(k,p_1)$ otherwise (the ``odd'' form, the conjugate by $t_{p_1,k+1}$); for $m=1$ the form is decided by the parity of the pattern of $(u(p_1),u(k),u(k+1))$, which is Lemma \ref{lemma:generaldetour}. When the level-$(k-1)$ pair has cycles with top $k+1$, these merge into $c$ and $d$ (as the letter $t_{13}$ merges into $\mathrm K_1,\mathrm K_1',\mathrm K_4,\mathrm K_4'$ for $k=2$), and we have not extracted a closed pivot formula beyond the canonical description by keys.

The identity \eqref{eq:Dk} determines \eqref{eq:Jk} only on the diagonal; since \eqref{eq:Jk} is symmetric in $z_1,z_2$ but of degree up to $k-1$ in each, the off-diagonal part is not a consequence of it, and for the off-diagonal part the only termwise formulation we have found is \eqref{eq:Bk}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why pairs, and why ``space'' is needed] \label{remark:medtheta}
The doubled formula is a statement about the quantization of a product of two elementary polynomials in the \emph{same} alphabet $x_1,\ldots,x_k$. Expanded in standard elementary monomials, such a product uses the alphabets $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ and $x_1,\ldots,x_{k+1}$ (see the determinant below). Consequently, in a product of several factorial elementary polynomials, $\theta$ acts as $\theta(\cdots)\cdot\theta\big(E_{p_1,k}E_{p_2,k}\big)\cdot\theta(\cdots)$ only if no other factor uses the alphabets $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ or $x_1,\ldots,x_{k+1}$, that is, only if the equal pair of columns is preceded by a column of height at least $k+2$. This is precisely the condition under which the medium theta $\medtheta(v^{-1})$ of Definition \ref{def:codetheta} retains an equal consecutive pair, and it is the reason the quantum algorithm iterates over $\medtheta(v^{-1})$ rather than over the sorted code $\theta(v^{-1})$ (which may contain equal parts without the required space) or the strict shape $\thop(v^{-1})$ (which has no equal parts at all and is therefore larger). Three or more equal columns would require the alphabets $x_1,\ldots,x_{k+2}$ and beyond, and the doubled formula has no analogue for them; accordingly $\medtheta$ never retains more than two equal parts, the remaining ones being incremented as in the construction of $\theta$.

Explicitly, the quantized product of two equal columns is a Giambelli-type determinant. For a Grassmannian permutation $w$ with descent at $k$ and shape $\lambda$ (so $\code(w)=(\lambda_k,\ldots,\lambda_1)$), the double Schubert polynomial is the determinant $\sch_w(x;z)=\det\big(E_{\lambda'_i+j-i,\,k+j-1}(x;z)\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq\lambda_1}$ of factorial elementary polynomials in the nested alphabets $x_1,\ldots,x_{k+j-1}$; every term of the expanded determinant is a standard elementary monomial, so $\theta$ acts on it term by term and gives the quantum Giambelli formula \cite{fomin1997quantum,ciocan1999partial}
$$\qsch_w(x;z)=\det\big(\qelem_{\lambda'_i+j-i,\,k+j-1}(x;z)\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq\lambda_1}.$$
For the $k\times2$ rectangle this reads
$$\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\theta\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2)\big)=\qelem_{k,k+1}(x;z)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)-\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z)\,\qelem_{k-1,k}(x;z),$$
whose first instances are
$$\qsch_{d_2[1]}(x;z)=\qelem_{1,2}(x;z)\qelem_{1,1}(x;z)-\qelem_{2,2}(x;z),\qquad
\qsch_{d_2[2]}(x;z)=\qelem_{2,3}(x;z)\qelem_{2,2}(x;z)-\qelem_{3,3}(x;z)\qelem_{1,2}(x;z).$$
The doubled formula computes the same product by two compatible Pieri steps in $k$ variables, with all coefficients manifestly positive, instead of by the two signed products of Pieri steps in $k$ and $k+1$ variables suggested by the determinant.
\end{remark}

\section{The Peterson--Woodward comparison formula} \label{section:pw}

In this section we prove Proposition \ref{prop:parabolic}. We use the notation of Section \ref{section:definitions}: $W_P$ is a parabolic subgroup of $S_n$ with $P=\pcomp P$, $\omega_P=w_P$ is its longest element, and for $\lambda_P\in Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P$ we write $\lambda_B\in Q^\vee$ for its Peterson--Woodward lift. In the language of roots, $\lambda_B$ is the unique lift with $\langle\alpha,\lambda_B\rangle\in\{0,-1\}$ for all positive roots $\alpha$ of the Levi subgroup of $P$; in type $A$ these are the roots $\alpha_i+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}$ with $s_i,\ldots,s_{j-1}\in P$, and $\langle\alpha_i+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1},d\rangle=\langle d\rangle_{ij}$, so this is the condition of Definition \ref{def:pwlift}.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition~2.10]{huang2015equivariant}}] \label{prop:huangli}
Let $W_P$ be a parabolic subgroup of $W$ and $u,v,w\in W^P$. Then for any $\lambda_P\in Q^\vee/Q_P^\vee$, we have that
$$c_{u,v}^{w,\lambda_P}(y;y) = c_{u,v}^{w\omega_P\omega_{P'},\lambda_B}(y;y),$$
where $\omega_P$ is the longest element in $W_P$, $\omega_{P'}$ is the longest element in $W_{P'}$, where $P'$ is the parabolic subgroup generated by $\{s_\alpha\mid \alpha\in\Delta_P,\langle \alpha, \lambda_B\rangle=0\}$, and $\lambda_B$ is the Peterson-Woodward lift of $\lambda_P$.
\end{proposition}

Here the left-hand side is a structure constant of $QH_T^*(G/P)$, i.e.\ of the parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials $\qsch^P_w(x;y)$ of Definition \ref{def:parabolic}, which represent the Schubert classes of $QH_T^*(G/P)$ \cite{lam2014quantum}, and the right-hand side is a structure constant of $QH_T^*(G/B)$.

\begin{corollary} \label{corollary:pwz}
With the notation of Proposition \ref{prop:huangli}, for all $u,v,w\in W^P$,
$$c_{u,v}^{w,\lambda_P}(y;z) = c_{u,v}^{w\omega_P\omega_{P'},\lambda_B}(y;z).$$
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The parabolic polynomials satisfy the same quantum Cauchy identity as in Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}. Indeed, by \cite[Lemma~18]{lam2014quantum}, $\qsch^P_w(x;y)=\theta^P(\sch_w(x;y))$ for $w\in W^P$, where $\theta^P$ is the parabolic quantization map of Ciocan-Fontanine \cite{ciocan1999partial}, extended linearly over the coefficient variables; applying $\theta^P$ to the classical identity $\sch_v(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}\sch_{v_1}(y;z)\sch_{v_2}(x;y)$ gives $\qsch^P_v(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}\sch_{v_1}(y;z)\qsch^P_{v_2}(x;y)$, where we note that $v_2\in W^P$ for every reduced factorization (see below), so that $\theta^P(\sch_{v_2}(x;y))$ is indeed $\qsch^P_{v_2}(x;y)$. Hence
$$c_{u,v}^{w,\lambda_P}(y;z) = \sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}c_{u,v_2}^{w,\lambda_P}(y;y)\,\sch_{v_1}(y;z).$$
Here, if $v=v_1\cdot v_2$ is reduced and $v\in W^P$, then $v_2\in W^P$: for $s_i\in P$, $v_2s_i<v_2$ would give $\ell(vs_i)\leq\ell(v_1)+\ell(v_2s_i)<\ell(v)$, contradicting $v\in W^P$. Thus Proposition \ref{prop:huangli} applies to each term, and resumming by Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary} gives
$$\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}c_{u,v_2}^{w\omega_P\omega_{P'},\lambda_B}(y;y)\,\sch_{v_1}(y;z) = c_{u,v}^{w\omega_P\omega_{P'},\lambda_B}(y;z).\qedhere$$
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition \ref{prop:parabolic}]
Let $d_P\in Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P$ with Peterson--Woodward lift $d=\lambda_B$. In type $A$, $\langle\alpha_i,d\rangle=2d_i-d_{i-1}-d_{i+1}=\langle d\rangle_i$, so the parabolic subgroup $P'$ of Proposition \ref{prop:huangli} is generated by $\{s_i\in P\mid\langle d\rangle_i=0\}$, that is, by $\{s_i\mid i\in z_P(d)\}$, and $\omega_P\omega_{P'}=w_Pw_{z_P(d)}$. The claim is therefore Corollary \ref{corollary:pwz}. (The passage from $S_n$ to $S_\infty$, i.e.\ from $P$ to $\pcomp P$, is the stability discussed after Definition \ref{def:parabolic}.)
\end{proof}
